APPENDIX F - Georgina Francis


========================================================================
APPENDIX E - Beatrice Helen Clee
========================================================================

--- APPENDIX F - Georgina Francis ---
In 1996, Mrs Georgina Francis was a patient of Shipman. She was 53 years of age and married. Mrs Francis contacted the Inquiry because she was concerned that Shipman might have tried to kill her at his surgery on Tuesday, 30th
April 1996. She attended his surgery on that day, suffering from abdominal pain. Shipman gave her an injection of what she now believes to have been diamorphine. Soon afterwards, Mrs Francis began to vomit. Shipman called an ambulance and Mrs Francis was taken to hospital. She was discharged the following day.
Mrs Francis says that this incident has always troubled her. However, it was not until she learned of Shipman’s practice when killing his patients and noticed similarities to her own experience that she became concerned. She contacted Tameside Victim Support and Witness Service and later raised her concern with her subsequent general practitioner (GP). Later, she asked to see her medical records and found that Shipman had recorded that, on 30th
April 1996, he had administered to her 10mg diamorphine intravenously.
In August 2002, Mrs Francis contacted Alexander Harris, Solicitors, in connection with her concerns. On 12th
August 2002, Alexander Harris wrote to the Inquiry, requesting that the circumstances of Shipman’s treatment of Mrs Francis be investigated.
The Inquiry has taken two statements from Mrs Francis and has obtained two reports from Dr John Grenville. The Inquiry has also had access to Mrs Francis’ GP and hospital records and to the surgery appointments sheet for the relevant date.
The GP records comprise both handwritten records and printouts of computerised records. It appears that Shipman used handwritten records for Mrs Francis until September 1993. There is then a gap until August 1994, when computerised records begin; they continue until shortly before Shipman’s arrest in August 1998. However, on some occasions (and 30th
April 1996 is one of them), Shipman has made entries in both the handwritten and the computerised records.
This decision has been written following consideration of the papers in the case. There is no conflict of evidence such as to render an oral hearing necessary or desirable.
The Medical Background
The medical records reveal that Mrs Francis consulted Shipman quite regularly for a variety of matters. She had a hysterectomy in about 1986 and was on hormone replacement therapy. That accounted for quite regular visits. Mrs Francis also suffered from tenosynovitis and pain in her knees. She had occasional coughs, colds and chest infections.
From an examination of the records, it does not appear that Mrs Francis often suffered from abdominal pain, although the episode of 30th
April 1996 was not the first occasion on which she had made a complaint of that nature. In particular, on 7th
June 1995, the records suggest that Mrs Francis consulted Shipman in his surgery complaining of abdominal pain. The relevant entry reads:
‘(D) Abdominal colic
Here (this practice)
...
soft ut soft bs active plus’
This suggests that Mrs Francis was suffering from abdominal pain, that Shipman examined her and found her abdomen and uterus to be soft; her bowel sounds were very active. There is no further reference to abdominal pain until 30th
April 1996.
Mrs Francis says that she consulted Shipman on a regular basis in connection with abdominal pain. He had prescribed Fybogel and Colofac, which, as Dr Grenville confirms, are used for the treatment of diverticulitis. Mrs Francis also says that Shipman would often turn up unannounced at her home. The medical records do not bear this out. However, it is quite possible that Shipman failed to record these consultations in the medical records. Mrs Francis thinks that Shipman might have regarded her as a nuisance patient. There is no indication in the medical records that that was so. However, it remains a possibility that he did.
The Day of the Incident
April 1996, after a sleepless night with severe stomach pains, Mrs Francis decided to consult Shipman at his surgery. Her husband drove her to the surgery later that morning. The surgery was extremely busy when Mr and Mrs Francis arrived and she had to wait to be seen. The practice appointments sheet confirms that Mrs Francis was seen in the open surgery that morning. It is not possible to tell from this record at what time Mrs Francis was seen although other documents to which I shall refer would suggest that it was at about 10.50am.
Mrs Francis recalls that she went into Shipman’s consulting room while her husband remained outside in the waiting room. She was taken into the examination room and lay on the couch while Shipman examined her abdomen. She recalls Shipman saying that he was going to give her an injection for her pain, although he did not say which drug he was going to use, nor did he inform Mrs Francis of his diagnosis. Mrs Francis cannot remember where Shipman administered the injection, although she thinks it was given in her arm. However, in her second statement, Mrs Francis says that she can remember that the injection was given
‘in one go’
and was not given gradually over a period of time.
Shipman’s record of the consultation in the Lloyd George cards (i.e. the cards on which GPs’ manuscript records are recorded) reads as follows:
‘(L) sided abdo pain
colicky
nausea no D or V
PU ok’
That suggests that Mrs Francis was complaining of pain on the left side of the abdomen; the pain was of a colicky nature. Mrs Francis was complaining of nausea but had not been suffering from diarrhoea or vomiting. She was passing urine satisfactorily. It is to be noted that Shipman did not record the fact that he had given any treatment. As Dr Grenville observes, that is very poor practice and, if it were to be established that he had given diamorphine, may give rise to a suspicion that Shipman did not wish it to be known that he had done so.
According to Mrs Francis, within a very short time of receiving the injection, she was violently sick. She says that Shipman appeared to panic and that he said he would need to call an ambulance. Mrs Francis continued to be sick until the ambulance arrived. The medical records contain a Greater Manchester Ambulance Service Patient Report Form, which records that the ambulance arrived at the surgery at 11.45am following a telephone call received at 11.33am. The call to the ambulance service was not a 999 emergency call.
Under the section headed
‘Mechanism of Injury or Medical History’
, the Patient Report Form records:
‘Renal colic
GP letter with details’
Under the comments section the following has been written:
‘Given 10mg
Diamorph
by GP at
10.50 hrs.
Now Vomiting.’
I infer from that entry that Shipman must have told the paramedics that he had given Mrs Francis 10mg diamorphine.
The referral letter written by Shipman confirms the story. It reads as follows:
‘D
diagnosis
renal colic
This woman started with (L) sided renal pain with colicky pain radiating down to the groin yesterday. She feels nauseated, no D or V,
O/E afebrile
Diagram of abdomen which Shipman has shaded on the left-hand side
very tender kidney easily felt
D - ?renal stone
HFS’
On the following page Shipman has written:
‘PH hysterectomy 1986.
DMP
Hormonin T OD
HFS
Rx
treatment
IV 10mg of Diamorph at 1050 hours’
Mrs Francis’ Admission to Tameside General Hospital
Mrs Francis was admitted to Tameside General Hospital (TGH). There is a detailed admission note, timed at 1.30pm. This records the history of the onset of abdominal pain with nausea and the fact that Mrs Francis had begun to vomit after being given an injection. The note does not specify which drug had been administered, although the admitting doctor must have known if he had read the referral letter. Mrs Francis recalls that a doctor asked her why Shipman had given her
‘this drug’
. At that stage, Mrs Francis did not know what drug had been administered to her.
Mrs Francis’ past medical history was noted. There was reference to appendicectomy and hysterectomy but no reference to a previous history of diverticulitis or any renal problem. The doctor’s examination confirmed pain and tenderness in the left loin and left lower quadrant of the abdomen. The admitting doctor made a provisional diagnosis of renal colic, possibly due to a renal stone, and arranged for a number of investigations to be carried out. The second entry in the hospital medical records on 30th
April (made by a different doctor) suggested a different diagnosis of a possible urinary tract infection or diverticular disease.
On 1st
May, Mrs Francis was feeling better. The tests had proved negative. Mrs Francis was discharged home. The discharge letter gave a diagnosis of
‘non-specific abdominal pain’
but the consultant suggested that the acute pain which had necessitated admission might be due to diverticular disease which would be best treated by a high fibre diet. Mrs Francis did suffer a recurrence of her abdominal pain and was readmitted to TGH the following month.
Medical Evidence
Dr Grenville says that the use of diamorphine for the control of acute colicky pain thought to be due to a renal stone was acceptable treatment in 1996, although, since the early 1990s, it has been thought preferable to use a non-steroidal anti-inflammatory agent such as diclofenac. I note that Shipman had given Mrs Francis diclofenac on one occasion in 1994 although it is not clear for what condition.
Dr Grenville also observes that, if diamorphine is to be used for the relief of acute pain, it should be given slowly and titrated against response. He says that to give 10mg quickly to a patient who is not suffering from any pain might depress respiration and might be dangerous.
Dr Grenville deplores Shipman’s failure to make a proper record of the administration of diamorphine in Mrs Francis’ medical records. He draws attention to the fact that Shipman apparently had diamorphine readily available, although he claimed that he did not keep any on the premises and did not keep a controlled drugs register.
Notwithstanding those criticisms, Dr Grenville says that he does not think that Shipman intended to kill Mrs Francis. Had he intended to do so, he would have given a large dose. A dose of 10mg might be dangerous given as a stat (‘one-off’) dose but the pain that Mrs Francis was experiencing and the vomiting may have served to keep her respiratory drive up. He accepts, of course, that the fact that Shipman claimed to have given 10mg diamorphine is not reliable evidence of the amount given. Shipman’s word cannot be relied upon. However, he draws attention to the fact that (apart from vomiting, which is a well-known side effect of diamorphine) Mrs Francis did not suffer any of the signs or symptoms associated with opiate poisoning.
Submission on Mrs Francis’ Behalf
It is submitted on Mrs Francis’ behalf that Shipman probably tried to kill Mrs Francis. Several
‘suspicious features’
are noted. These are:
Shipman’s regular unannounced visits at her home
the fact that she was never given a diagnosis of renal colic
the fact that Shipman could have written anything between administering the injection and the arrival of the ambulance
the ‘stat’ dosage
the possibility that Shipman regarded her as a
‘nuisance patient’
It is pointed out that, by April 1996, Shipman was an accomplished killer and had killed several patients in his surgery.
There is no doubt that, by 30th
April 1996, Shipman was an accomplished killer. Moreover, he had already killed three patients in his surgery at Market Street, Hyde, and was to kill another, Mrs Edith Brady, on 13th
May 1996.
That said, it seems to me most unlikely that Shipman intended to kill Mrs Francis. There are a number of reasons for so saying.
First, Mrs Francis was not suffering from any life-threatening disease. She had no history of heart disease and her blood pressure was at a very good level. The vast majority of Shipman’s victims were suffering from a condition to which the death could reasonably be attributed. If Shipman were to have killed Mrs Francis, he would have had to explain her death to her husband. As she was only 53, this would have been difficult. Shipman usually killed patients whose deaths were relatively easy to explain.
Second, Shipman was so experienced a user of diamorphine for the purpose of killing that it is almost inconceivable that, if he had intended to kill Mrs Francis, he would have failed to do so. Although I acknowledge that one cannot accept his word that the dose given was 10mg, in fact, I think that probably was the dose given. The only adverse effect the diamorphine had was to make Mrs Francis sick. Mrs Francis does not say whether the diamorphine relieved her pain but I think it must have done. As Dr Grenville says, the dose was more than enough to achieve that. However, despite the fact that the dose was given ‘stat’, it apparently did not affect Mrs Francis’ respiration and certainly did not affect her level of consciousness. Those factors are consistent with the effect of a 10mg dose on a patient who was suffering from pain and who was vomiting. I infer that the dose probably was 10mg. There is certainly no evidence to suggest that Shipman gave 30mg, which was his preferred dose when intending to kill. That amount would almost certainly have rendered Mrs Francis unconscious and would probably have killed her.
The other
‘suspicious features’
advanced on Mrs Francis’ behalf are not, in my view, probative. Shipman made unsolicited visits to many patients and did not kill them. Indeed, most of the patients that he killed had requested a visit on that day or were being ‘re-visited’ following a request a few days earlier. It is said that Shipman did not tell Mrs Francis of his provisional diagnosis. He might have done and she might have forgotten; it would be surprising if he had not told her. But even if he did not, I cannot see that that suggests that he intended to kill her. I agree that the fact that Shipman did not enter the diamorphine injection in the medical records and added it as an afterthought to the letter of referral suggests that Shipman might have wished to conceal the fact that he had given diamorphine. He might well have wished to conceal that, not because he had used the drug for an improper purpose but because his possession of that drug was unlawful.
Finally, Mrs Francis suggests that Shipman may have regarded her as a nuisance patient. I cannot say what Shipman thought about Mrs Francis but, as I have said, there is no hint in the records that Shipman thought she was a nuisance and it does not appear to me from her records that her attendances were more frequent than those of many other patients of her age.
In conclusion, I feel confident that I can reassure Mrs Francis that there is no reason to suspect that, when Shipman gave her diamorphine on 30th
April 1996, he was seeking to do anything other than relieve her pain.
To view the evidence relating to this case, please
APPENDIX G - Alfred Wright


========================================================================
APPENDIX F - Georgina Francis
========================================================================

--- APPENDIX G - Alfred Wright ---
Mr Alfred Wright died at his home, 13 Gair Street, Hyde, on Tuesday, 31st
March 1987 at the age of 63. He was a patient of one of the doctors at the Donneybrook practice, Hyde, where Shipman was in practice in 1987. Dr Ian Napier, who was a colleague of Shipman at the Donneybrook practice, certified that Mr Wright’s death was due to carcinomatosis due to a carcinoma of the bronchus.
The police did not investigate the death and there has been no inquest.
In April 2003, the Inquiry was contacted by Mr Carl David Wright, Mr Wright’s son, who expressed concern about the circumstances surrounding his father’s death. Mr Wright’s medical records have not survived. The Inquiry has, however, had access to the copy entry in the register of deaths (‘death certificate’), the cremation forms relating to the death, the Donneybrook practice duty doctor diary for the period and Shipman’s own visits book for 1987. The Inquiry has also received a letter from Dr Napier.
This decision has been written following consideration of the papers.
The Evidence
Mr Carl Wright told the Inquiry that his father was diagnosed with lung cancer about six months before he died. In about January 1987, Mr Wright was admitted to hospital in order to have one of his lungs removed. During the course of the operation, it was discovered that the cancer had spread to other organs and the operation was abandoned. It is clear, therefore, that Mr Wright had carcinomatosis.
Despite his condition, Mr Wright apparently remained quite active up until his death. He would often go to the shops and he would also go out for a drink either by himself or with his son. Mr Carl Wright was in contact with his father every day to see how he was.
According to Mr Carl Wright, his father did not appear to deteriorate noticeably following the operation. Although Mr Wright appeared
‘fed up’
with the situation, he was still eating and drinking.
Mr Carl Wright now thinks that his father’s general practitioner (GP) was Shipman. He recalls that his mother’s doctor was Dr Jeffery Moysey (another of the Donneybrook doctors) and that his mother and father had different doctors, although both were based at the Donneybrook practice.
The Circumstances Leading to Death
On the evening of 30th
March 1987, Mr Carl Wright visited his father. Mr Carl Wright thinks that he arrived at his father’s house at about 6pm or maybe slightly later. He found his father watching television in the lounge. Mr Wright said that he was in pain. Mr Carl Wright telephoned the Donneybrook practice to request a visit from a doctor. Mr Carl Wright cannot recall exactly what time this was, but thinks it was around 6pm to 6.30pm. It would certainly have been after normal working hours and the ‘on-call’ duty rota would have been in operation. The partners took it in turns to deal with evening calls received from patients of any of the doctors in the practice. However, if the doctor on duty wished to make use of a commercial deputising service, he was free to do so (at his own expense). The duty doctor diary for 30th
March 1987 shows that Dr Napier was on duty that night and that he transferred calls to the deputising service at 7.45pm.
The doctor arrived at the house whilst Mr Carl Wright was in the kitchen preparing a sandwich and a drink for his father. Mr Carl Wright thinks that the doctor arrived
‘well after’
7pm. Mr Carl Wright thinks that the doctor was Shipman. It appeared that Mr Wright and the doctor knew each other quite well. Mr Carl Wright has told the Inquiry that he believes that the doctor who came was his father’s usual doctor. However, it is clear beyond doubt that Mr Wright’s usual doctor was Dr Moysey. That was clearly stated on cremation Form A, which was signed by Mr Carl Wright himself. That, of course, does not mean that the doctor who attended on the evening of 30th
March could not have been Shipman. However, that seems most unlikely as it was Dr Napier who was on duty. There does not seem to be any reason why Shipman should have been involved.
The doctor examined Mr Wright whilst the latter was seated in the lounge and Mr Carl Wright went back into the kitchen. As he brought the food and drink to his father, the doctor handed him a prescription and said something like
‘I’ll give him an injection which will kill it, so you don’t need to rush with the tablets’
. Mr Carl Wright then went back into the kitchen to tidy up and he assumes that his father was then given an injection. He thinks that it was given in the top of the leg.
Mr Carl Wright says that, after clearing up the kitchen, he returned to the lounge and accompanied the doctor to the door. He recalls telling his father that he would telephone in the morning to see how he was. He recalls leaving his father sitting in a chair watching television. He had put the drink and the sandwich on a coffee table. Mr Wright locked the front door as he left. Mr Carl Wright and the doctor then left the house together. As they were leaving, the doctor said that there was nothing to worry about and that his father’s complaint was
‘just a twinge’
At about 10am the following day, Mr Carl Wright telephoned his father but there was no reply. He continued to telephone his father throughout the morning, but failed to get a response. At about 2pm to 2.30pm, Mr Carl Wright went to his father’s house. He let himself in and found his father seated in exactly the same position as he had been in the night before. The drink and sandwich which had been prepared were left untouched and the television was still switched on. The fire was on full and the room was
‘red hot’
Mr Carl Wright telephoned the surgery. After a while, Dr Napier arrived. In his witness statement, Mr Carl Wright says that he is 100% sure that it was not Dr Napier who had attended on the previous evening. He recalls telling Dr Napier that a doctor had attended the night before. Dr Napier cannot remember attending Mr Wright in the evening or examining his body the following day.
Certification
The death certificate shows that the cause of death was certified by Dr Napier as carcinomatosis due to a carcinoma of the bronchus.
Dr Napier completed cremation Form B. He stated that death had occurred at approximately 10am on 31st
March 1987. Dr Napier said that he was not the usual medical attendant of the deceased but he had attended the deceased during his last illness for a period of one day. Dr Napier said that he last saw the deceased alive 12 hours before the death and that he saw the body 24 hours after death. He wrote that the mode of death was
‘syncope’
(which suggests a faint or sudden loss of consciousness), based on
‘Own observation’
. He continued,
‘Son left for work in morning
Found dead in afternoon’
. He did not state what was the duration of the ‘syncope’. Dr Napier recorded that Mr Wright was nursed during his last illness by his son.
Dr Napier has examined cremation Form B and has confirmed that he completed it. As I have said, he has no recollection of visiting Mr Wright. However, he says that he has no reason to suppose that the statements he made are incorrect. I have some difficulty in understanding how Dr Napier could claim to have observed Mr Wright’s syncope but that is not relevant to the question of Shipman’s possible involvement in the death.
In the absence of Mr Wright’s medical records, it is difficult to reach any firm conclusion about the circumstances of his death. However, it seems to me that it is unlikely that Shipman played any part in it at all. First, I am satisfied that Shipman was not Mr Wright’s usual GP. Mr Carl Wright himself had said on cremation Form A that his father’s usual GP was Dr Moysey. Second, Dr Napier was on duty in the evening of 30th
March and one would expect it to have been he who attended Mr Wright following the request from his son. Third, there is no reason to think that Dr Napier would have deliberately made false entries on cremation Form B. There are differences between the entries on Form B and Mr Carl Wright’s recollection of events. There are two possible explanations for these differences. Mr Carl Wright’s recollection of events could be faulty; Dr Napier might have misunderstood Mr Carl Wright’s account of events when they spoke on the occasion when Dr Napier attended to examine the body. However, I do not think that there is any possibility of error in respect of the entry in which Dr Napier said that he had seen Mr Wright 12 hours before his death. Dr Napier would say that from his own knowledge. He would have known whether he had been to the house on the night before the death. Dr Napier’s statement would not depend upon information received from Mr Carl Wright. It seems to me that the evidence strongly suggests that it was Dr Napier who visited Mr Wright during the evening of 30th
March. In any event, there is no evidence to suggest that the doctor who attended was Shipman and I feel able to reassure Mr Carl Wright that there is no need for him any longer to feel any concern that Shipman might have played a part in his father’s death.
APPENDIX H - John (or Jonathon) Harkin


========================================================================
APPENDIX G - Alfred Wright
========================================================================

--- APPENDIX H - John (or Jonathon) Harkin ---
The Background
On 19th
July 2002, the Inquiry published its First Report, in which I set out my findings about the number of his patients whom Shipman had killed. I found that Shipman had killed 215 patients, including the 15 patients of whose murder he had already been convicted. I found that 210 of the deaths investigated by the Inquiry had occurred as a result of natural causes. There were 45 deaths in respect of which I felt that there was a real suspicion that Shipman might have been responsible for the death, although the evidence was not sufficiently clear for me to reach a positive conclusion. In addition, there were a further 38 deaths in respect of which there was so little evidence, or evidence of such poor quality, that I was unable to form any view at all. The total number of deaths with which I dealt in the First Report was, therefore, 508. The First Report, and the findings contained in it, received a great deal of publicity in the media.
Mr Harkin’s Letter
On 19th
September 2002, almost nine weeks after publication of the First Report, I received a letter from Mr John (elsewhere referred to as Jonathon) Harkin, a former prisoner at Her Majesty’s Prison (HMP) Preston (Preston Prison). In his letter, Mr Harkin informed me that, while in prison, he had been a ‘listener’, trained by the Samaritans to assist other prisoners who were in distress and/or at risk of committing suicide. The purpose of the ‘Listener Scheme’ was to provide prisoners who were in a distressed or vulnerable state with someone to talk to. For five weeks in September and October 1998, Shipman was on remand at Preston Prison, having been arrested and charged with the murder of Mrs Kathleen Grundy and other related offences on 7th
September 1998. Mr Harkin said that he had acted as a listener for Shipman.
In his letter, Mr Harkin said that he had seen Shipman on at least eight occasions and, on one occasion, had stayed overnight with him in his cell. He described how, once, after Shipman had been questioned and charged with further murders, he returned to his cell in a
‘very agitated and psychotic state’
. Mr Harkin claimed that Shipman had told him,
‘more as a boast than a confession’
, that the police
‘knew nothing’
and that he had
‘taken 508 lives’
. Mr Harkin said that Shipman had described his actions as
‘acts of God’
and had said that he had acted as he had done because
‘he had the power’
Mr Harkin said that he was
‘overwhelmed’
by what Shipman had told him, although he was aware from his experience as a listener that inmates in a vulnerable state could make
‘wild and exaggerated claims’
. He explained that it was only as the criminal trial and the Inquiry progressed that he had realised that what Shipman had told him
‘could have been significant’
. The criminal trial ended in January 2000 with Shipman’s conviction. The Inquiry began its investigations in January 2001. Hearings began in June 2001 and continued to the end of the year, and the Inquiry’s investigations into the deaths continued until publication of its First Report which was, as I have said, in July 2002.
Mr Harkin concluded his letter by saying that he had had to
‘reflect on the ethics of breaching listener confidentiality’
, but felt that it was right to do so in these
‘exceptional circumstances’
. He indicated that he was happy to speak to the Inquiry further if required.
Press Stories about Mr Harkin’s Claims
A week later, while the Inquiry was considering Mr Harkin’s letter, articles appeared in both a local and a national newspaper, containing essentially the same account as that given in Mr Harkin’s letter to the Inquiry, together with some additional details. Mr Harkin’s reason for
‘going public’
and speaking to the press was said in the local newspaper article to be a wish to pass on the information that he had to the families of those people whom Shipman had killed. He said that they needed to know the truth.
The Inquiry’s Investigations
The Decision to Investigate
Having regard to my findings in the First Report, it seemed inconceivable that Shipman could have killed as many as 508 of his patients. Nevertheless, the Inquiry team decided that Mr Harkin’s claim that Shipman had made such an admission to him must be investigated and evaluated. The possibility obviously existed that Mr Harkin’s claim was untrue or, at the least, exaggerated. It was necessary first to discover whether Mr Harkin had ever acted as a listener for Shipman as he claimed. If it appeared that he had done, the Inquiry wished to discover whether there was any reliable documentary or witness evidence that Mr Harkin had written down or told anyone about the number of deaths allegedly mentioned by Shipman at the time that Mr Harkin’s dealings with Shipman were said to have taken place or, at any rate, before publication of the First Report.
Seeking Evidence from Various Sources
The Inquiry team made contact with Mr Harkin and attempted to obtain a witness statement from him, together with any documentary and witness evidence that might support his account. In addition, information was sought from staff at Preston Prison about the dates when Mr Harkin and Shipman were in custody and about Mr Harkin’s activities as a ‘listener’. The Inquiry also made contact with the Samaritans in an attempt to ascertain whether any of their volunteers had had any previous knowledge of Mr Harkin’s claim that Shipman had taken 508 lives.
The Sequence of Events
Enquiries of Preston Prison revealed that Mr Harkin had been remanded in custody to the prison on 22nd
April 1998, having been charged with two offences of rape, one of threatening to kill and one of unlawful imprisonment. His trial took place in November 1998. He was convicted of some offences and sentenced to seven years’ imprisonment. He was released on parole in December 2001.
Shipman arrived at Preston Prison on 9th
September 1998, two days after he had been arrested and charged with Mrs Grundy’s murder. He was placed on ‘D’ wing, the wing where Mr Harkin was being held. Shipman was immediately placed on ‘suicide watch’, which meant that he was considered to be at risk of committing suicide and that his cell was checked by prison staff every 20 minutes. On 5th
October 1998, Shipman was interviewed by the police about three further murders; he became distressed and confused and the interview had to be discontinued. On his return to Preston Prison, he was transferred to the hospital wing where he remained for the remained of his stay at the prison. Two days later, on 7th
October 1998, he was charged with the three additional murders. On 14th
or 15th
October 1998, Shipman left Preston Prison and was transferred to HMP Liverpool. If Mr Harkin was correct in his claim that he had seen Shipman when he returned to his cell after he had been questioned by the police and charged with additional murders, that occasion must have been on 7th
October (when he was charged with the further murders). It is possible, however, that the meeting referred to took place on 5th
October, after Shipman had been questioned by the police and at a time when he knew that he was likely to be charged with further offences but before he had actually been charged with them. If Mr Harkin did indeed see Shipman on one of these dates, he must have done so on the hospital wing.
Attempts to Obtain Evidence from Mr Harkin and Members of His Family
The Inquiry’s attempts to obtain a witness statement from Mr Harkin were not successful. He was traced and interviewed by the Solicitor and Deputy Solicitor to the Inquiry on 8th
October 2002. At the interview, Mr Harkin stated that he had in his possession a notebook in which he had recorded details of the times and dates of his contacts with Shipman and details (in code, to prevent the contents being read by prison staff) of what had been said during listening sessions with him. Mr Harkin explained that it was usual practice for listeners to keep such notes in respect of all their listening activities. It had been arranged that Mr Harkin would provide relevant material from his notebook (which he had not shown to the Solicitor and Deputy Solicitor to the Inquiry when they interviewed him) in support of his evidence.
Following the interview, a draft statement, based on information given at the interview, was prepared and sent to Mr Harkin on 11th
October 2002. He was asked to consider it and to advise the Inquiry of any amendments he wished to make. He did not respond or return the statement. On 7th
November 2002, the Inquiry wrote to Mr Harkin, requesting him to sign the statement. The following day, a document was delivered to the Inquiry by hand; this was in the form of a handwritten statement from Mr Harkin. It did not address many of the questions that had been put to him in interview and contained very little relevant material over and above that which had appeared in his original letter. It was clear from correspondence enclosed with the handwritten statement that Mr Harkin had been in contact with an organisation known as the HM Prison Service Safer Custody Group (‘the Safer Custody Group’) in connection with the signing of a statement for the Inquiry and that they had offered support and assistance to him in deciding whether to provide a signed statement. His probation officer was also aware of the situation and prepared to assist. The correspondence contained an offer by a representative of the Safer Custody Group to approach the Inquiry team on Mr Harkin’s behalf. In the event, no such approach was made.
Meanwhile, the draft statement that had been prepared by the Inquiry had still not been returned. On 12th
November 2002, the Inquiry wrote to Mr Harkin, reminding him that he had agreed to provide information from his notebook to be attached to his statement and asking whether it was his intention to sign the draft statement. No response was received. On 27th
November 2002, the Inquiry wrote to Mr Harkin, enclosing a copy of the letter of 12th
November 2002 and enquiring whether it was his intention to provide a statement in a form that was acceptable to the Inquiry and that dealt with the relevant issues. No response was received from Mr Harkin and no statement or any other information was ever provided. As a consequence, the only direct evidence the Inquiry has about Mr Harkin’s dealings with Shipman consists of that contained in his original letter received on 19th
September 2002 and in his handwritten statement.
The Inquiry also attempted to take witness statements from Mr Harkin’s father and brother, with whom it had been suggested that Mr Harkin had discussed his conversations with Shipman shortly after they had taken place. At first, it appeared that Mr Harkin senior and his son would be prepared to assist but they subsequently changed their minds and declined to provide statements to the Inquiry.
The Listener Scheme
A witness statement was obtained from Mr Dean Corbishley, Suicide Prevention Co-ordinator at Preston Prison. In 1998, it had been part of Mr Corbishley’s role to provide a link between the Samaritans and the Listener Scheme at Preston Prison. The role of a listener was fulfilled by prisoners who had been trained for the purpose. The Samaritans organised the training for listeners. At Preston Prison, assistance was also given by a probation officer and by Mr Corbishley. The listeners’ training course took place over three days. At the end of the third day, a prisoner completing the course would be given a badge and a certificate confirming that he had completed the training successfully. Listeners wore badges so that they were easily identifiable by other prisoners.
An important feature of the Listener Scheme was that confidentiality was assured. Listeners were taught that they should not divulge information communicated to them in their capacity as listeners. They were told that the only situation in which they could betray confidentiality was if a prisoner told them that s/he intended to commit a murder or if s/he disclosed information about a planned terrorist attack. Otherwise, confidentiality was guaranteed. It was, however, permissible for listeners to share confidential information given to them in their listening capacity with other listeners and with representatives of the Samaritans. Representatives of the Samaritans visited Preston Prison weekly in order to provide support to the listeners there. These weekly meetings provided an opportunity for listeners to discuss any concerns they had or difficulties they encountered. The meetings were confidential and prison staff were not permitted to attend. Within the prison, Mr Corbishley would deal with any practical difficulties encountered by listeners.
A considerable amount of listening activity took place on an informal basis and would not be organised by - or necessarily known to - the prison staff. A prisoner might seek out a listener at a meal time or at some other time when prisoners were free to associate and a confidential listening session might take place in those circumstances. At other times, the sessions would be more formal. Members of the prison staff would request a listener to spend time with a particular prisoner who it was thought might benefit from the listener’s services or who had specifically requested them. On occasion, if a prisoner was distressed and wanted company, a listener would be permitted to stay overnight with the prisoner in his cell. Mr Corbishley told the Inquiry that no record would be made by the prison staff of listening sessions that took place, or of occasions when a listener stayed with a prisoner overnight. The Inquiry has been told that, when a prisoner specifically requested a visit from a listener, this was generally noted in the prisoner’s records. Shipman’s prison records, which the Inquiry has seen, disclosed no such request during the relevant period. However, it does not appear that the absence of a note in the records can necessarily be taken to mean that no such request was made.
Mr Harkin’s Listening Activities
Mr Corbishley told the Inquiry that he had checked his records to ascertain when Mr Harkin had undergone his training as a listener. He said that those records showed that Mr Harkin had been trained over the three-day period from 6th
to 8th
October 1998. Shipman had been moved to the hospital wing on 5th
October, i.e. (if Mr Corbishley’s records were correct) before Mr Harkin began his training. Mr Corbishley said that the Samaritans insisted that only prisoners who had completed their training should act as listeners. He could not rule out the possibility that an untrained or partially trained listener might have had a listening session with a prisoner in the sort of informal circumstances I have previously described. However, he thought that it was unlikely that a listener who had not completed his training would have received a formal request from a member of staff to act as a listener for a prisoner, particularly one who was as ‘high profile’ as Shipman and a known suicide risk. He also thought it was unlikely that a listener who had not completed his training would have been permitted to stay overnight with a prisoner. However, he did not entirely exclude the possibility. He thought it unlikely that a listener who had not completed his training would have been asked to assist a prisoner who was housed on the hospital wing. If, however, Mr Harkin had acted as a listener for Shipman before the completion of his training and had built up a rapport with Shipman, Mr Corbishley said that it was likely that he would have been sent to assist Shipman after he had finished his training.
Mr Harkin’s Dealings with Shipman
Mr Harkin’s letter to the Inquiry, and the newspaper articles that followed, gave the impression that he was an experienced listener at the time of his contact with Shipman. It is plain that that was not in fact the case. Indeed, it is probable that he did not complete his training until 8th
October 1998, although there is, I suppose, a possibility that Mr Corbishley’s records are wrong. What then of Mr Harkin’s claims about his contacts with Shipman? I have already mentioned that, if he saw Shipman on the occasion when Shipman returned to his cell after having been questioned and/or charged with further murders, that must have been on 5th
October (when he was interviewed by the police) or on 7th
October (when he was charged with the further murders). According to Mr Corbishley’s records, Mr Harkin was not fully trained until 8th
October and should not have been permitted to act as a listener before that time. In his handwritten statement, Mr Harkin said that he had had listening sessions with Shipman on as many as seven occasions before the time when the 508 deaths were mentioned. This would suggest (according to the records) that he had begun his listening activities even before his training had started. The question arose, therefore, whether Mr Harkin’s claim that he had been asked to act as a listener for Shipman was true and, if so, whether he could have done so as early as 5th
or 7th
October 1998, or even before.
Mr Corbishley told the Inquiry that he had had a conversation with Mr Harkin about Shipman. That conversation had taken place around the time that Shipman was convicted in January 2000. Mr Harkin had observed to Mr Corbishley that he was extremely pleased that Shipman had been convicted because Shipman had told him things that had made him
‘very uncomfortable’
. Mr Corbishley did not ask Mr Harkin what he meant by this. However, he considered that the conversation indicated that Mr Harkin must have acted as a listener for Shipman on at least one occasion.
Evidence from the Samaritans
When interviewed by the Inquiry’s representatives, Mr Harkin claimed that, shortly after Shipman had disclosed to him that he had taken 508 lives, he had told two Samaritan volunteers about the disclosure. The Inquiry approached the Samaritans and asked whether this was the case. Discussions as between listeners and Samaritan volunteers are, of course, confidential. After taking advice from others within the organisation, one of the volunteers involved, Ms Patricia Parkinson, agreed to provide a limited amount of assistance by giving a statement that did not, in her view, breach confidentiality. In a witness statement to the Inquiry, Ms Parkinson said that she was one of the Samaritan volunteers supporting Mr Harkin during and after Shipman’s trial. It is clear from Ms Parkinson’s statement that there was some discussion between Mr Harkin and her about Shipman’s crimes. However, she said that she had no recollection of any specific number of crimes being mentioned by Mr Harkin. She said that the phrase used by him was
‘the tip of the iceberg’
. This is a very unspecific phrase. It could mean that Mr Harkin was aware because of something Shipman had told him that it was likely that Shipman was guilty of many more crimes than those with which he was charged. On the other hand, it could just be an impression gained by Mr Harkin from press reports and other sources that it was unlikely that the whole story of Shipman’ s criminality had yet emerged. The significant fact about Ms Parkinson’s account is that she has no recollection of a number of crimes being mentioned. If it had been - and if the number had been as many as 508 - one would have expected the fact to stick in her mind.
Ms Parkinson’s evidence is also significant in another respect. The fact that Mr Harkin was thought to be in need of support from Samaritan volunteers during Shipman’s trial and afterwards seems to me to be explicable only by the fact that he had indeed had some previous dealings with Shipman. I infer that those dealings must have been in connection with Mr Harkin’s listening activities.
It is unfortunate that Mr Harkin has declined to co-operate with the Inquiry by responding to the questions put to him. The result of this is that the Inquiry has little more information about his claims than that which appeared in his original letter. Nevertheless, I am able to reach certain conclusions about those claims.
I accept that Mr Harkin did act as listener for Shipman at some point and that he was asked to do this by a member or members of the prison staff. It is not clear to me how often he did so and this does not matter. It seems to me likely that he was asked to act as Shipman’s listener before he had completed his training and when both he and Shipman were housed in ‘D’ wing. If that is right, his first contact with Shipman must have been before 5th
October, when Shipman was moved to the hospital wing. I think it unlikely that Mr Harkin would have been asked to act as a listener before he had undergone any training at all. It may be, therefore, that he underwent some part of his training earlier than Mr Corbishley’s records would suggest.
Although the Inquiry has been told that (for reasons I entirely understand) the Samaritans take the view that prisoners should not act as listeners until they are fully trained, it seems to me quite possible that members of the prison staff did not always comply with this requirement, especially if the demand for listeners exceeded the number of fully trained listeners available. I note also Mr Corbishley’s evidence that Mr Harkin got on well with the prison staff, that he made himself helpful whenever possible and that he was, in the opinion of Mr Corbishley and some others within the prison, a ‘model prisoner’. It may also be that, because of Shipman’s personality, there was some difficulty in identifying a prisoner suitable to act as his listener. It seems to me that these factors make it more likely that the prison staff might have ‘bent the rules’ and allowed Mr Harkin to act as a listener before he had completed his formal training. If he had acted as a listener for Shipman on ‘D’ wing, it is quite possible, it seems to me, that he might later have been chosen to act as his listener on the hospital wing, whether or not he had completed his training.
I accept also that, while Mr Harkin was acting as listener for Shipman, Shipman may well have made comments that Mr Harkin found disturbing, particularly in the light of what was revealed about Shipman subsequently. That would explain why Mr Harkin was thought to require support during and after Shipman’s trial and why he made the comments he is reported to have made to Mr Corbishley and to Ms Parkinson. There is no doubt that Shipman was in a highly emotional state at and after the police interview on 5th
October and it might be that he said things that he would not have said if he had been in full control of himself. However, I do not accept Mr Harkin’s assertion that Shipman claimed that he had taken 508 lives. I do not believe that any number was mentioned. Nor do I think that Shipman made anything that amounted to a ‘confession’ of his crimes to Mr Harkin. I think that Mr Harkin might have gained the impression, from a combination of what Shipman said and what was reported in the press, that Shipman had killed a number of his patients, more than the four with whose deaths he had been charged in October 1998 and perhaps even more than the 15 of whose murders he was eventually convicted. But I do not accept that matters went any further than that. Nor, in my view, can any reliance be placed upon Mr Harkin’s account of precisely what was said during his sessions with Shipman.
I reject Mr Harkin’s claim that Shipman mentioned a figure of 508 deaths for two main reasons. First, my detailed examination of the deaths of Shipman’s patients leaves me completely confident that Shipman could not have killed as many as 508 of them. There were many cases in which the evidence was such that it was quite clear that Shipman had played no part in the death. There were others where it was highly probable that this was the case. Moreover, the statistical analyses performed by Professor Richard Baker in the course of his clinical audit of Shipman’s practice could not support an excess of deaths of as many as 508.
My second reason for rejecting the claim is the inherent unlikelihood that a figure stated to Mr Harkin by Shipman should have been identical to the number of deaths dealt with by the Inquiry in its First Report. This would be an extraordinary coincidence. It seems to me much more likely that Mr Harkin adopted the figure of 508 from the First Report, published only a short time before, and was thus seeking to suggest that every death examined by the Inquiry had in fact been caused by Shipman. As I have said, I am completely certain that this was not the case. Why Mr Harkin should have chosen to act in this way is not clear. It may be that he was seeking attention; the fact that he chose to tell his story to the press would suggest that. He might have been seeking to gain financially from his involvement with Shipman, although I note that the article that appeared in the local newspaper stated that he had received no fee for the story. The reason he gave for
‘going public ’
- namely a desire to assist the families of Shipman’s victims - was unconvincing. Indeed, it was difficult to see how his claims could possibly have assisted those families who, only a few weeks previously, been given full written decisions by the Inquiry in respect of their relatives’ deaths and who were in the process of coming to terms with my findings. On the contrary, his claims were likely to cause yet further distress.
It is significant that, when Mr Harkin was asked by the Inquiry to address a number of issues in a written statement and to produce the contemporaneous documentation which he claimed would support his assertions, he declined to do so. He suggested to the Inquiry that he was under pressure from the authorities not to give a statement to the Inquiry. I can understand that those supervising his parole licence might have been unhappy about him speaking to the press. However, I cannot believe that they would have seriously objected to his giving evidence in order to assist a judicial Inquiry; indeed, as I have said, it was evident that he was offered support in connection with making a statement to the Inquiry. Nor do I believe that Mr Harkin’s reluctance to give further information to the Inquiry was caused by concerns about confidentiality. No such concerns had prevented him from speaking to the press.
Whatever Mr Harkin’s reasons for making the claims that he did, I am confident that his central claim - namely that Shipman indicated to him that he had taken 508 lives - was untrue. As I have said in
Chapter Seven
of this Report, I believe the true total, although shocking, to have been much lower.
To view the evidence relating to the issues raised in this chapter, please
