d) Deaths during paediatric posts
Shipman appears to have spent 19 months in paediatric posts, first as a senior house officer for 6 months (August 1971 to January 1972), then as a registrar for 13 months (August 1972 to September 1973). He issued 10 (43.5%) of the 23 MCCDs issued by doctors in paediatric posts in the five-year period August 1969 to July 1974, and therefore information from cremation Form B completed by doctors in paediatric posts is presented here. There were cremation certificates relating to 14 of the 23 deaths (Table 4.25).
Table 4.25.Number of cremation certificates issued by Shipman and other doctors at PGI when working in paediatric posts.
Of the seven patients for whom Shipman issued a cremation certificate, six were aged under one year old, the other being aged 62. Of the seven patients for whom the other doctors issued cremation certificates, two were under one year old, one was aged one, one aged 11, and the other three were aged 51, 57 and 71. It is likely, therefore, that doctors in paediatric posts at PGI had occasional responsibility for adult medical care as well. If the adult patients are excluded, Shipman reported being the only person present at the deaths of three children, and as being present with nurses and relatives at the deaths of the other three (i.e. he had been present at all the deaths). Of the four cases certified by the other doctors, nurses alone were present at the deaths of three, and nurses with a doctor and relatives were present at one. The mode of death was reported as being sudden or syncope in two cases by Shipman and in two cases by the other doctors. In four cases, Shipman indicated the mode as coma, the other doctors using this term for two cases. The duration of dying is shown in Table 4.26.

========================================================================
Appendix: Review of Surviving Records
========================================================================

--- Conclusions ---
In the five years under investigation, 1637 MCCDs were issued by Shipman and the other doctors. Cremation certificates were identified for 766 deaths, 46.8% of all deaths. Since the information contained on Form B is not available for more than 50% of all deaths, some caution is required in assuming that the patients who were cremated are representative of all the patients who died. The possibility of an unrecognised difference between those who were cremated and those who were not should not be overlooked. Nevertheless, it is important to note that the survival and identification of such a high proportion of cremation certificates more than 30 years after they were completed have provided crucial evidence to this review of deaths at PGI. Much is owed to the diligence of the Inquiry team in obtaining the cremation forms.
The evidence from the cremation forms presents a consistent pattern that bears alarming similarities to the pattern of the certificates Shipman completed in later years when he was a GP in Hyde. They indicate that:
Shipman was more likely to be present at the death of his patients, the forms indicating him as present either alone or with other people at 50% of deaths; this finding is reflected in evidence that Shipman reported attending his patients before death more recently than did his colleagues and that he examined the body sooner after death.
Of the six infants for whom Shipman completed cremation certificates, he reported being present at all deaths, and was alone at the deaths of three.
These findings are reminiscent of the findings of the analysis of Shipman’s cremation certificates completed years later when he was working as a GP, and raise serious concern about his activities as a junior hospital doctor.


========================================================================
APPENDIX A - Harold Shipman and Deaths at Pontefract General Infirmary, 1969 to 1974. A Review of Medical Records, Death Certificates and Cremation Forms
========================================================================

--- Appendix: Review of Surviving Records ---
In the following cases, information has been extracted from the copies of clinical records stored on microfiche. In many cases, substantial proportions of the records had not survived or had not been copied to microfiche. In each case, brief information about the primary cause of death and the doctor certifying death is included. Information from cremation certificates is then provided for those cases for which a cremation certificate has been found. The clinical history obtained from the records is then outlined, followed by observations taking account of all the information available.
1. Margery/Marjory Bateman.
Born 04/08/1915.
Died 25/08/1970, Age 55.
Cause of death 1: secondary carcinoma of liver
Certifier: HFS
History
The available records relate to excision of the right eye in 1964. A preliminary pathology report indicated a diagnosis of malignant melanoma of the uvea. The records contain no information about the condition causing death, care prior to death, or the circumstances of death.
Observations
It is not possible to form any conclusions in this case.
2. Gladys Teal.
Born 05/02/1920.
Died 28/09/1970, Age 50.
Cause of death 1: carcinomatosis
Certifier: HFS
Cremation information
Time of death: 05.45
Last seen alive: 27.9.1970
Present at death: Sister Inman
Mode & duration: coma, 2 hours
Seen after death: 20 minutes
History
Mrs Teal was admitted on 17th
September 1970 from the outpatient department following investigation of lower abdominal pain and a lower abdominal mass. Laparotomy on 21st
September 1970 revealed a large abdominal mass. A biopsy was taken. On 27th
September 1970 she was seen by a junior doctor because of vomiting. Bowel sounds were heard, she was not dehydrated, and Stemetil was commenced. On 28th
September 1970 a junior doctor was called to see the patient to confirm death at 5.45am.
The biopsy taken at laparotomy confirmed a poorly differentiated adenocarcinoma, possibly ovarian or endometrial in origin.
The discharge letter was written by Shipman and it confirms that he was the doctor to see the patient post-operatively and had prescribed the Stemetil. The nursing records confirm the information in the medical records. Post-operatively, intramuscular pethidine and then oral Distalgesic tablets were used for analgesia. The patient’s husband had been told that the diagnosis was inoperable carcinoma. Abdominal pain continued and the patient was managed with Omnopon and Fentazin. Nursing records indicate that the patient remained ill, collapsing at 5am on 28th
September 1970.
Observations
Having discovered inoperable cancer at laparotomy, subsequent clinical management was conservative. This would have been in accordance with practice at the time. The records do not contain evidence to cause suspicion about what happened.
3. Albert Sidebottom.
Died 16/11/1970, Age 91.
Cause of death 1: coronary thrombosis
Certifier: HFS
History
Mr Sidebottom was admitted at the request of his general practitioner on 11th
November 1970 with acute retention of urine. The handwriting of the admitting doctor is not that of Shipman. The recorded history on admission indicates a previously reasonably fit 91 year old man with acute retention of urine. An electrocardiogram (ECG) was reported as showing bundle branch block, although a copy of the ECG was no longer included in the microfiched notes. A urinary catheter was passed. The letter from the consultant surgeon to the GP reports that prostate cancer was suspected and therefore stilboestrol was started.
On 16th
November Dr W Belk noted seeing the patient at 4pm. Mr Sidebottom had chest pain, and was pale and shocked. His blood pressure had fallen to 80/40. He was given oxygen and morphine 15mg (the drug chart has not survived, and precise information about the administration of morphine is not available). Later the same day, Shipman made an entry in the records. He reported being called to the patient at 8pm, arriving at 8.03pm to find the patient dead. He recorded the cause of death as coronary thrombosis and carcinoma of the prostate, and also added the name of SN (i.e. staff nurse) Steales. It is not clear what the name of the staff nurse is intended to indicate.
Observations
The patient was elderly, and the occurrence of a major cardiovascular event when admitted to hospital with another condition would not be unexpected. Shipman does not appear to have encountered the patient until after the death had occurred. The case presents a potential explanation for the finding of a greater number of certificates being issued by Shipman than the other doctors at the hospital. He may have had a policy of completing a MCCD even when he had not been the attending doctor. In the case of Mr Sidebottom, he could have left the certificate to be completed by another junior doctor, but according to the records he completed the certificate himself.
4. Ada Hardacre.
Born 21/02/1885.
Died 03/12/1970, Age 85.
Cause of death 1: acute peritonitis
Certifier: HFS
Cremation information
Time of death: 00.15
Last seen alive: 2.12.70?
Present at death: no one
Mode & duration: coma, 2-3 hours
Seen after death: 5 minutes
History
Mrs Hardacre was a patient of Headlands (the geriatric unit). In November 1970 she had been transferred to PGI for above knee amputation of the left leg because of peripheral gangrene. The nursing records made following the amputation suggest she was relatively frail, but she made a slow recovery. She was admitted from Headlands again on 30th
November 1970 with abdominal pain and vomiting. It is not possible to identify the handwriting of the admitting doctor. On admission, the patient was found to be ill, and to have a large, tender swelling in the right lower abdomen. An initial diagnosis of constipation was made, but notes made by another doctor (presumably the more senior doctor on call) suggest that this diagnosis was overruled since antibiotics were prescribed. A letter sent to the GP by the surgical registrar in December indicates that the diagnosis was presumed to have been an appendix mass, which was treated conservatively. Conservative management would have been a reasonable plan in a frail, elderly patient.
The medical notes contain very little additional information until 3rd
December, when Shipman writes that he was called to see the patient at 12.25am. He confirmed death, and noted the cause of death as appendicitis, with secondary causes including the recent amputation. The nursing records confirm that the patient was very ill on 2nd
December and that an injection of 50mg pethidine had been given. On 3rd
December, the nursing notes indicate that pain had continued, and that 10mg morphine had been given the previous evening. The patient’s condition had continued to deteriorate, with death occurring at 12.15am. An autopsy examination recorded the cause of death as acute peritonitis secondary to acute pyonephrosis.
Observations
The autopsy confirms the diagnosis of peritonitis. The attribution of this to appendicitis by the medical team is understandable and I do not think it is material to the outcome. Conservative treatment would have been a reasonable option, and opiate analgesia would have been appropriate. The principal decision on the patient’s management was to treat conservatively rather than operatively, and this was taken by senior staff and not Shipman. Concern about Shipman’s role does not appear to be appropriate in this case.
6. Thomas H Griffiths.
Died 04/03/1971.
Certified cause of death 1: bronchopneumonia
Certifier: HFS
History
The records available relate to the follow-up at PGI of a myocardial infarction, dated to 1969. There is no information about Mr Griffiths’ final illness, or any record entries made by Shipman.
Observations
None possible.
7. Ada Cawthorn.
Died 08/05/1971, Age 92.
Cause of death 1: myocardial infarction
Certifier: HFS
History
Mrs Cawthorn was admitted on 7th
May 1971 at the request of her GP. The GP’s letter was not included with the microfiched documents. The notes indicate that Shipman saw the patient on admission. He found her to be confused, and obtained the history from her daughter. That morning, the patient had become ill, with rolling of the head and vomiting, having previously been in good health. The principal findings on examination were confusion, a slow pulse, blood pressure 110/60, and upgoing plantar reflexes. Shipman thought that the diagnosis was either a stroke or a myocardial infarction. Routine blood tests, a midstream specimen of urine, chest x-ray and ECG were ordered. The following day, the ECG was noted as indicating a posterior myocardial infarction. Initial treatment was
‘G.N.C.’
(presumably meaning general nursing care), and Largactil 80mg intramuscularly if needed. The drug records were not among the microfiched documents, and therefore it is not clear whether or not the patient received Largactil.
Shipman records being called to see the patient at 7.55pm on 8th
May, the patient having died ten minutes earlier. He recorded the cause of death as myocardial infarction. The nursing records tend to bear out the medical notes, and state that the patient collapsed and died at 8pm on 8th
May.
Observations
The diagnosis of myocardial infarction cannot be confirmed with certainty since the microfiched documents do not include the ECG. It is not clear whether or not Largactil was administered, and the records are brief. Nevertheless, the history is consistent with the diagnosis.
8. Edith Mary Swift.
Born 14/06/1921.
Died 09/05/1971, Age 49.
Cause of death 1: acute bronchitis
Certifier: HFS
History
Mrs Swift was admitted at the request of her GP on 7th
May 1971, the admission history being taken by Shipman. She had developed a respiratory infection a few days before admission and had then become breathless. She was diagnosed as having a chest infection and bronchitis. On examination, she was cyanosed, short of breath with a respiratory rate of 30 per minute, and with widespread rhonchi and crepitations reported in the chest. She commenced antibiotics, aminophylline, Lasix, salbutamol and oxygen. A more senior doctor appears to have started streptomycin (an intramuscular antibiotic) as well. On 8th
May 1971 she had improved. No entries were made in the medical records until 9th
May 1971 when Shipman had already been called to the patient to confirm death. The notes indicate that the patient was confused in the night, but had slept well.
The nursing records indicate that the patient was started on streptomycin as well as the other medication. The patient was reported as being noisy and confused, although still breathless. The presence of confusion raises the possibility of hypoxia and would suggest that the infection must have been a severe one. Sedation was prescribed, including nitrazepam at night and phenobarbitone during the day. The nursing records indicate that the patient collapsed and died on 9th
May 1971 at 7am.
The records include a report of an autopsy, which gives the cause of death as acute bronchitis and pneumonia. The autopsy disclosed oedema and consolidation in the lungs.
Observations
The patient must have had a severe respiratory infection, and Shipman’s initial management was reviewed by a more senior doctor, a second antibiotic being added to her treatment. She appears to have collapsed and died fairly suddenly. There is nothing from the records to indicate that the terminal collapse was preceded by attendance by Shipman, and consequently the records do not give rise to concern about his actions.
9. James Richardson.
Died 20/06/1971, Age 65.
Cause of death 1: Cerebrovascular Accident (CVA)
Certifier: HFS
Cremation information
Time of death: 20.45
Last seen alive: 45 minutes
Present at death: Sister Walsh
Mode & duration: coma, 45 minutes
Seen after death: 5 minutes
History
Mr Richardson was admitted on 19th
June 1971, having been found on the floor at home and brought to the hospital casualty department. A history of blackouts was noted. The admitting doctor was Shipman. Twenty four hours later Shipman noted that the patient had collapsed suddenly. Shipman did not recall the presence of major neurological signs such as unilateral limb weakness, but noted that the blood pressure was unrecordable and that tone had been reduced in all limbs. He concluded that the patient had had a cerebrovascular accident. No statement is made about the patient’s conscious level. The patient died after 45 minutes, the death being certified as due to cerebrovascular accident with epilepsy as a secondary factor.
The nursing records suggest that the patient had been on the floor at home for some time, possibly days. He had been dehydrated and had signs caused by prolonged pressure. He was reported to have had a minor blackout on the day of admission. Later in the day he was reported as confused and incontinent of urine. A note on 20th
June 1971 indicates that the patient had no movement on the left side of the body, a finding suggestive of a cerebrovascular accident. The nursing records confirm that he deteriorated suddenly, after or in association with a rise in temperature, the principal feature of deterioration being loss of consciousness. The deterioration does not appear to have been related to attendance by Shipman, as the nursing record indicates that he was called after the patient had become unwell.
Observations
The precise cause of death is not clear. A cerebrovascular accident is a possibility. Although the typical features are not fully described in the medical records, the nursing records do suggest that a stroke may have occurred on 20th
June 1971 prior to the loss of consciousness. The condition that led to the admission to hospital is not clear. The patient was found on the floor, but the reason why this occurred does not seem to have been established. The records did not suggest that his death occurred after attendance by Shipman and consequently concern about the death is not justified, although there may be some uncertainty about the exact clinical cause. It is quite possible that the patient did have a stroke on 20th
June, the first sign being left-sided weakness. The loss of consciousness and death could be explained by extension of the stroke.
10. Bernadette McCourt.
Born 05/01/1972.
Died 07/01/1972, Age 2 days.
Cause of death 1: prematurity
Certifier: HFS
History
This baby was delivered on 5th
January 1972 at 31 weeks’ gestation. The midwife appears to have thought the baby had died, but 35 minutes after birth she was noted to be breathing and was therefore placed in an incubator. Shipman’s records indicate the baby had a variable respiratory rate of between 10 and 20 per minute (rather slow) but had a good colour and a normal pulse rate. My photocopy of the records up to 7th
January 1972 is incomplete, but it looks as though progress was reasonable until the morning of 7th
January 1972. Death was recorded at 6pm on 7th
January. The blood test results are compatible with respiratory difficulties.
Observations
Survival at 31 weeks’ gestation in 1972 would have been relatively uncommon. The records do not give rise to suspicion about Shipman’s actions, and clinical management appears to be fairly typical of care in a district maternity hospital at that time. However, please note the poor photocopy of the records available to me.
11. Cissie Macfarlane.
Born 18/06/1914.
Died 29/02/1972, Age 57.
Cause of death 1: renal failure
Certifier: HFS
History
Mrs Macfarlane was referred by her GP to a consultant surgeon because of right-sided abdominal pain and haematuria. Outpatient investigations indicated severe kidney problems, including failure to pass contrast media at intravenous pyelogram, raised erythrocyte sedimentation rate (ESR) and urea, and some anaemia. Approximately two weeks after she was first seen in outpatients, an admission was arranged.
The patient was admitted on 24th
February 1972. The initial notes in the records are not signed, but they do indicate that the patient had significant problems. These included rapid onset of symptoms, which had become progressively worse. Her ankles were swollen, the patient was breathless, pale, sweaty and she looked anaemic. Her blood pressure was high (230/140). There were widespread inspiratory and expiratory noises in the chest. The jugular venous pressure was raised. She had oedema to the knees. Initial treatment included ampicillin, oxygen, intramuscular frusemide, and intravenous aminophylline. The initial potential diagnoses included chronic renal disease, possibly haemolytic uraemic syndrome, and bronchopneumonia.
Subsequent treatment included transfusion of two units of blood on 25th
February 1972.
No further entries were made in the medical records until 29th
February 1972, when Shipman recorded the patient as dead at 9.10am, giving the cause of death as renal failure, cause not known. As far as I can determine, Shipman had not made an entry in the medical record prior to that date. No information about clinical management and clinical course between the dates of 25th
and 29th
February 1972 is contained in the medical records.
The nursing records indicate that Mrs Macfarlane continued to receive aminophylline supplemented by steroids. She remained very ill, and it certainly appears as though the relatives were aware of this since her daughter stayed overnight on 26th
and 27th
February. On 27th
February, the nursing notes state that the patient was gravely ill and seemed to be deteriorating.
Observations
Mrs Macfarlane had rapidly progressive renal failure. This was clearly established, although the precise cause of the condition was never identified. She was assessed by a specialist and was attended by several doctors, and Shipman was involved only after the patient had died and in order to confirm the death. There does not appear to be any reason for concern about unlawful killing by Shipman in this case.
12. Gertrude Shepherd.
Born 19/09/1898.
Died 06/03/1972, Age 73.
Cause of death 1: coronary thrombosis
Certifier: HFS
History
Mrs Shepherd had a long history of asthma and bronchitis, with several past hospital admissions for acute exacerbations and episodes of treatment with steroids. She had an acute myocardial infarction in November 1970, followed by a probable second infarction in December 1971. She was admitted again on 3rd
March 1972. The handwriting and signature of the admitting doctor are not those of Shipman. The features at admission were severe pre-cordial chest pain that had started the day before, and the recent onset of impaired consciousness. She had been given 100mg pethidine by the GP prior to admission. A diagnosis of posterior myocardial infarction was made.
Shipman made a note in the records of seeing the patient on 4th
March, when he stated that there was no chest pain, although he found crepitations and rhonchi in the lungs. The next entry is dated 6th
March, and was made by Shipman. He stated the patient had died, giving the time as 11.10am, and reported making a full examination. He signed a MCCD with the diagnoses coronary thrombosis (
‘C.T.’
) and chronic bronchial asthma. The records did not include a copy of the nursing notes or the drug chart.
Observations
It is quite clear that Mrs Shepherd had ischaemic heart disease and chronic respiratory problems. She was severely ill when admitted. The records provide very little information about care after admission, and it is therefore difficult to reach firm conclusions about what happened. However, the records do not mention features that would give rise to concern.
13. William Turner.
Died 06/03/1972, Age 52.
Cause of death 1: myocardial infarction
Certifier: HFS
Cremation information
Time of death: 15.05
Last seen alive: 6.3.72
Present at death: HFS
Mode & duration: cardiac arrest, minutes
Seen after death: 5 minutes
History
Mr Turner was admitted on 5th
March 1972, having seen his GP because of breathlessness and chest pain. After admission, initial treatment involved diuretics and lignocaine. The records did not include a copy of an electrocardiogram (ECG) or of serial cardiac enzyme levels. Nevertheless, the history recorded tends to support the diagnosis of acute myocardial infarction in someone with known coronary heart disease.
On 6th
March 1972 at 2.35pm, the patient was noted to have fibrillation. Cardioversion was attempted, extra cardiac massage was started and bicarbonate infusion was given. There appears to have been some initial response, with the return of a heart rhythm, but only 30 per minute. However, the heart is recorded as stopping again and the patient was certified dead at 3.05pm. The entries in the records are signed by Shipman, but the clinical history of acute myocardial infarction followed after an interval by collapse and death is not unusual.
The nursing records confirm that Mr Turner was experiencing extra systoles, and that he arrested on 6th
March. The attempted resuscitation is noted.
The use of intravenous lignocaine by infusion, as recorded on the input and output chart, does suggest that he had been found to have ventricular extra systoles, which indicate increased risk of ventricular fibrillation. Lignocaine was the standard treatment for this problem at that time. The resuscitation appears to have been reasonable and the temporary return of some heart rhythm should not, I think, be regarded as unusual or a matter for concern.
Observations
Shipman was present at death. During resuscitation, he would normally have been accompanied by others taking part in the resuscitation, although no one else was mentioned as present on the cremation certificate. Either others had been present, but for some reason Shipman chose not to record this fact, or Shipman had administered a lethal injection before the patient’s collapse, and was referring to this as the moment of death on the form. It would be interesting to know who had been present when the cardiac arrest was noted. There are no other features to raise suspicion in this case.
14. Albert Maeer.
Born 11/08/1914.
Died 15/03/1972, Age 57.
Cause of death 1: myocardial infarction
Certifier: HFS
History
Mr Maeer was known to have coronary heart disease, having had one previous infarct. He was admitted on 8th
March 1972 at 5.30am with a fairly typical history of pre-cordial chest pain and sweating with dizziness. His pulse was rather slow and irregular on admission, but appeared to settle by 9th
March. His progress seems to have been uneventful until 15th
March at 10.45am when he collapsed, the heart stopped and he stopped breathing. Resuscitation was commenced, involving extra-cardiac massage, intubation and intravenous adrenaline. These measures were not successful and he was confirmed dead at 11.50am. The entry relating to the resuscitation was made by Shipman. The ECG dated 8th
March 1972 is consistent with the diagnosis of myocardial infarction. The nursing records are consistent with the history as confirmed in the medical notes.
Observations
There is nothing in the records to suggest that Shipman administered a lethal injection prior to the patient’s collapse. The management was as would have been expected in 1972.
15. Butterfield Hammill.
Died 04/04/1972.
Cause of death 1: ventilatory failure
Certifier: HFS
History
Mr Hammill was admitted to PGI on 27th
March 1972, being transferred to Ackton on 31st
March. He was on the waiting list for admission to the geriatric unit at Headlands Hospital. Shipman states on a chest x-ray request form that the admission was for a
‘social problem’
. The GP’s letter indicates that admission was discussed in a telephone call between the GP and a doctor at PGI (presumably Shipman). The patient had a long history of chronic bronchitis, and had had two episodes of bronchopneumonia and a
‘mini-stroke’
recently during the winter. Symptoms suggestive of uraemia appeared 12 days before admission, and feeding through a Ryle’s tube was commenced. However, on the day of admission he had refused food and drink.
Shipman recorded the diagnoses on admission as
‘social problem’
‘chronic bronchitis’
. Two attacks of bronchopneumonia had been treated by the GP in the previous three months. The patient had become more confused in the previous few days; the GP had been unable to obtain an admission to Headlands. The patient was recorded as
‘not a good historian’
, and some vesicular breath sounds were noted, although there were few or no other positive findings recorded on examination. Routine blood and urine tests, a chest x-ray and an ECG were ordered, the Ryle’s tube was removed, and Shipman wrote a letter to the consultant at Headlands reporting the patient as mildly confused and ambulant. His wife had rheumatoid arthritis and was incapable of looking after him.
The investigation results are reported in a letter from Dr Meikle to the GP written after the patient died. The chest x-ray showed pulmonary fibrosis, emphysema and right basal bronchiectasis. There was a rise in white cells in the blood (a leucocytosis of 11.8), and the ESR was raised (52), findings that are consistent with an infection. The presumed diagnosis mentioned in the letter was chronic bronchitis. There is no indication in the records that the patient received treatment for the bronchitis; in particular no antibiotics are mentioned. The microfiche records do not include a copy of the drug chart, but a decision to prescribe antibiotics would normally be written in the continuation records. It is recorded in Shipman’s handwriting on 31st
March 1972 that the patient was started on 100mg of Largactil at night for sedation since he was confused.
The information about the death is very limited. No information is given about deterioration before death, other than the brief mention of Largactil. On 4th
April, Shipman records
‘pt died ... full examination ... 1a ventilatory failure 1b chronic bronchitis’
. This entry in the records is signed by Shipman. The letter written by Dr Meikle to the GP on 6th
April suggested that the reason for admission was chronic bronchitis, and that the patient’s condition seemed satisfactory.
Observations
There are reasons for concern about the true cause of death in this case. The patient appears to have had a respiratory infection that was not treated, and this is the most likely explanation for the confusion that occurred in the days before death. Administration of Largactil (chlorpromazine), especially at the relatively high dose of 100mg, would have aggravated matters, and might have encouraged the development of bronchopneumonia. Shipman makes no record of any deterioration in the patient’s condition, and the information about the death is minimal. Finally, he uses a vague term as one of the causes of death (ventilatory failure). It should be noted that the prescription record and nursing notes were not included in the microfiched records, and it is possible that these would have contained additional information to explain Mr Hammill’s care, but on the evidence available, it appears as though care was inappropriate. It is not possible to determine whether the inappropriate care was malicious, or whether it was simply poor.
16. Thomas Cullumbine.
Born 11/09/1917.
Died 12/04/1972, Age 54.
Cause of death: ventilatory failure
Certifier: HFS
History
Mr Cullumbine had a long history of chronic bronchitis and emphysema, and had been a patient of the chest clinic at PGI for some years. His respiratory problems deteriorated in March 1972. He was admitted on 14th
March with an exacerbation of his chronic bronchitis and was treated with antibiotics, prednisone and aminophylline. Shipman’s first recorded encounter with the patient was on 21st
March, when he noted
‘Bed situation very tight, wanted to transfer patient from Gateforth (bleeding ulcer) to PGI’
. Presumably this does not refer to Mr Cullumbine, but to a patient in Gateforth. Shipman records that
‘Pt
i.e. Mr Cullumbine
refused to go to Gateforth took own discharge’
. Mr Cullumbine was sent home taking an antibiotic, prednisone, Lasix, Valium, Slow-K, and aminophylline suppositories.
He was readmitted on 23rd
March at the request of his GP, Shipman being the admitting doctor. The patient was unwell, with a respiratory rate of 30 per minute, poor air entry to the lungs, and widespread noises in the chest. He continued prednisone, Lasix, Slow-K, Alupent and salbutamol. His peak flow rate was recorded as 70 litres per minute (a very low rate). The patient appears to have discharged himself again on 7th
April 1972. Several doctors had recorded entries in the medical records prior to this, but there was no entry by Shipman other than the entry on admission.
Mr Cullumbine was admitted again on 8th
April with the same respiratory problems. Additional information obtained on admission (the doctor was not Shipman) included panic attacks and bouts of temper, both of which exacerbated the respiratory problems. Management with aminophylline, prednisone, Alupent, salbutamol and Valium continued. On 11th
April signs of heart failure were noted, including a gallop rhythm, basal crepitations, raised jugular venous pressure and ankle oedema. Digoxin appears to have been commenced, although this was crossed out.
On the 12th
April, an entry is made by Shipman for the first time during this admission. He notes the congestive heart failure, including the signs noted the previous day. At 10.30pm, he records seeing the patient again. Mr Cullumbine was cyanosed, and Shipman gave him 80mg Lasix intravenously and 500mg aminophylline intravenously, indicating that the latter was given very slowly over ten minutes. By 11pm the patient had settled. At 11.10pm he was restless, and was given 250mg aminophylline slowly (some text here has been crossed out, and the original words cannot be determined with confidence). It should be noted that the prescription chart includes
‘morphine 10mg im stat dose’
(i.e. to be given intramuscularly when needed) on 12th
April, written up by Shipman. The appearance of the words that have been crossed out are consistent with morphine, although the dose and route of administration is uncertain. At 11.30pm the patient was recorded by Shipman as settled and sleeping, and at 11.40pm he had gradually stopped breathing. The patient was recorded as dying at 11.40pm, the cause of death being given as respiratory failure, emphysema and congestive heart failure. The nursing records contain no information about the circumstances of death.
Observations
The patient was clearly seriously ill with chronic bronchitis, emphysema and congestive heart failure. He was also distressed by his situation, and had discharged himself from hospital on two occasions. He was attended by Shipman at intervals during his care, and at his death. Shipman’s management of the patient’s last moments is curious. Aminophylline was used routinely in the 1970s to treat bronchospasm, but was used with care since it can disturb the heart rhythm, sometimes with fatal consequences. The usual dose would be around 250mg, given slowly intravenously. Shipman records giving 750mg within a 40 minute period. This dose could have been sufficient to cause death. The words that are crossed out and the prescription written by Shipman for morphine raise other possibilities. Shipman notes that the patient quickly fell asleep after the second injection, although this would be unlikely in someone who had been very short of breath, and within ten minutes he had died. This sequence of events is consistent with death following intravenous administration of a lethal dose of morphine. The death occurred in the evening. There must be serious concern about the true cause of death in this case.
17. Agnes Davidson.
Born 28/10/1898.
Died 14/04/1972, Age 73.
Cause of death 1: LVF
Certifier: HFS
Cremation information
Time of death: 22.15
Last seen alive: 14.04.72
Present at death: HFS
Mode & duration: collapse, 10-15 mins
Seen after death: present at death

The microfiched records contain no information about an admission to PGI in the 1970s, or about the patient’s death. They deal with episodes in 1958 and 1964.
Observations
It is not possible to form a view in this case. However, the cremation certificate completed by Shipman indicates he was the only person present at death, and that death was relatively quick, being described as preceded by ‘collapse’, and occurred in the evening. These features inevitably give rise to some concern.
18. Elizabeth Thwaites.
Born 30/04/1897.
Died 14/04/1972, Age 74.
Cause of death 1: congestive heart failure
Certifier: HFS
History
Mrs Thwaites was admitted in 1971 with heart failure and anaemia. A barium meal confirmed a duodenal ulcer, and she improved after a blood transfusion. She was admitted again to PGI on 6th
April 1972. The admitting doctor was Shipman, and the admission had been requested by the GP because the patient had congestive heart failure, was anaemic, and had melaena (had been passing blood). Shipman reported that the patient was breathless, had ankle oedema, looked anaemic, had a heart murmur with a thrill, and melaena stool on rectal examination. Shipman prescribed diuretics and digoxin, and organised blood and urine tests and a chest x-ray.
A note dated 10th
April made by a doctor other than Shipman (the nursing notes suggest the doctor was Dr Meikle) records the patient as sufficiently improved to be able to sit out of bed. On 12th
April, Shipman made four entries in the notes. On the first occasion he reported the patient as being
‘not very good today’
, with crepitations in the lung bases and ankle oedema. At 9pm, he thought the patient had left ventricular failure and administered 40mg frusemide intravenously with some improvement. However, at 10pm the patient was in a
‘collapsed state’
, and Shipman was not clear what was happening. He wondered if the cause was a stroke. There was no further entry about this episode, however, other than reporting an equivocal plantar reflex in one foot and an upgoing reflex in the other on 14th
April. On this occasion, Shipman made notes in relation to an iron infusion, estimating the dose required as 1000mg iron. A test dose appears to have been administered initially as required.
At 9.45pm on the same day, Shipman reported that the patient was
‘very poorly’
. There were crepitations in both lung bases and a gallop rhythm, both being features of heart failure. Shipman recorded the administration of 40mg Lasix intravenously and 0.25mg digoxin intramuscularly. At 10.30pm, Shipman wrote
‘pt died’
, stating the cause of death as anaemia secondary to a chronic duodenal ulcer, congestive heart failure and uraemia. He also stated
There is some corroboration of the history from surviving investigation results from the period of the final hospital admission. These include two positive occult blood tests and a serum iron level of 20 micrograms/100ml. The presence of a duodenal ulcer had been confirmed by a barium meal examination the previous year. The GP’s letter dated 6th
April also confirms the history prior to admission. The nursing records confirm the deterioration on 12th
April, and the continued poor condition in the following two days. The nursing records do not contain details about the further deterioration on 14th
April.
Observations
The patient was clearly severely ill. The precise cause for the deterioration in the patient’s condition on the evening of 12th
April is uncertain, and the only record of the terminal events is that made by Shipman. It is therefore difficult to form a reliable view about the circumstances of death. The events may have been as described by Shipman, but he was the principal witness, and he ended his contribution to the patient’s care by recording that the death was not reported to the coroner.

20. John Brewster.
Died 28/04/1972, Age 84.
Cause of death 1: LVF
Certifier: HFS
Cremation information
Time of death: 20.55
Last seen alive: ‘yes’
Present at death: HFS
Mode & duration: coma, 40-50 minutes
Seen after death: within 10 minutes
History
Shipman’s notes made on admission indicate that Mr Brewster was admitted on 28th
April 1972 at the request of his GP (Dr Roberts), and the notes contain a copy of the letter from the GP to Shipman in confirmation. Dr Roberts, who had spoken to Shipman by telephone before writing the letter, states that Mr Brewster had had a myocardial infarction previously, and that he now had symptoms suggesting another infarction. There was a history of pain, and findings of crepitations in both lung bases and some tenderness on the left side of the abdomen.
On admission, Shipman notes the past history of myocardial infarction and a history of Parkinsonism. The notes are headed with
‘Headlands refusal’
, underlined twice, indicating that the patient had been refused a bed in Headlands Hospital. Shipman’s notes reiterate the GP’s history - left-sided abdominal pain three days before admission, followed in the 24 hours prior to admission by a chest pain going into the left arm, breathlessness and feeling ‘poorly’. His respiratory rate was recorded as raised (36/minute, which is quite fast), with poor air entry, and crepitations and rhonchi in both lungs. Pulse was fast at 140 per minute, blood pressure rather low (100/70), and there was a gallop rhythm and palpable liver. The notes indicate that routine blood tests were ordered (full blood count, ESR, urea and electrolytes, cardiac enzymes and liver function tests), and a chest x-ray and an ECG. No results of any of these investigations are available in the records. Treatment is recorded as intramuscular morphine, digoxin, Lasix (i.e. frusemide), and Slow-K. This treatment would have been appropriate in 1972, although there is no information about the doses given, or the route of administration of the Lasix.
The next entry written later the same day by Shipman begins
‘2055 hrs’
. It goes on:
‘pt died. No need to report to coroner. DC 1 (a) LVF, (b) CT, (c) IHD 2. Parkinsonism’
. There is no information about the patient’s response to the initial treatment, no description of deterioration in condition, and no mention of attempted resuscitation.
Shipman wrote a letter to Dr Roberts dated 4th
May 1972, in which he notes that the patient had been refused admission to Headlands Hospital, and that he had deteriorated while
‘on the road’
(presumably while being transported to hospital by ambulance). No information about the final stages of the illness were given other than that he
‘died later on the day of admission’
, and that death was certified as due to LVF and coronary thrombosis.
Observations
In this case, the records contain worrying features that give rise to suspicion about the true cause of death. It is almost certain that the patient did have a myocardial infarction and LVF since a doctor independent of Shipman - the GP - had diagnosed these problems. However, the reasons for concern are:
Shipman places stress in the records that the patient was refused admission to Headlands Hospital. The reasons are unclear, but in the early 1970s there could well have been a reluctance of some hospitals to admit elderly patients if they were very short of beds. The way in which Shipman has underlined
‘Headlands refusal’
suggests that he was annoyed.
Shipman’s notes do suggest that the patient was ill, and had significant signs of LVF. In 1972, a dose of intravenous diuretic (frusemide) would usually have been administered by the doctor without delay, but the records do not make this clear.
The information about the death is minimal. It is worrying that no information is given about a deterioration in the patient’s condition or any action taken in response. No resuscitation is mentioned. The remark
‘no need to report to coroner’
is typical of record entries associated with unlawful killings during Shipman’s career in general practice.
Shipman states on the cremation certificate that he was present at death. He does not record the presence of anyone else at the death. The remark he makes about when he last saw the patient alive was
‘yes’
. The death occurred in the evening.

22. Agnes Scott.
Died 10/05/1972.
Cause of death 1: CVA
Certifier: HFS
Cremation information

Mode & duration: coma, 12 hours
Seen after death: immediately
History

May 1972. She had complained of chest pain the night before the GP had been called and morphine had been administered. The next morning there was still some pain, although initially she had appeared improved. However, she suddenly became much worse, with cyanosis, Cheyne-Stokes respiration and loss of consciousness. Shipman was the admitting doctor, and he reported finding constricted pupils, neck stiffness, and upgoing plantar reflexes. An ECG revealed bundle branch block. 23. James Rhodes.
Died 22/05/1972, Age 71.
Cause of death 1: coronary thrombosis
Certifier: HFS
Cremation information
Time of death: 21.25
Last seen alive: 22.5.72
Present at death: no one
Mode & duration: coma, 20-30 minutes
Seen after death: immediately
History
Mr Rhodes was admitted on 22nd
May 1972 with chest pain. It is not clear whether the initial clerking was undertaken by Shipman, but this does note the past history of coronary thrombosis, with little to find on examination, and the plans to investigate and observe. On 22nd
May 1972 he was seen by Shipman and was administered intravenous diuretic for heart failure (at 1pm). In 1972, intravenous injections would usually have been administered by a doctor rather than a nurse. At 9.25pm on 22nd
May 1972, the patient was found dead by a nurse. Shipman issued a MCCD, and the cremation certificate records him as having made an immediate examination of the body.
The records contained the result of a serum glutamic oxaloacetic transaminase test in the normal range, although this is probably not of significance. An ECG dated 22nd
May 1972 indicates sinus rhythm and no other major changes. It might be worth obtaining a specialist view on the ECG, but it should be noted that an ECG may not necessarily show major changes in the early stages of myocardial infarction.
The nursing records confirm the history of chest pain typical of cardiac ischaemia and the fact of death on 22nd
May 1972. No comment is made about the patient being found dead.
Observations
It is difficult to reach a conclusion about precisely what happened in this case. As far as can be judged from the records, Shipman did not see the patient immediately before death, and the injection of diuretic several hours before death was probably not relevant. An injection of an opioid or, for example, potassium would have produced death more quickly. Injection of a drug with prolonged adverse effects might have caused death, and it should be noted that the available investigations do not appear absolutely to confirm myocardial infarction. On the cremation certificate, Shipman recorded examining the body immediately after death, a statement that might suggest he could have been present at death, although he also noted that no one was present at death. The death occurred in the evening. These reservations should be noted, although there is probably little to cause serious concern in this case.

28. Joseph Evans.
Born 30/05/1912.
Died 31/07/1972, Age 50.
Cause of death 1: LVF
Certifier: HFS
History
Mr Evans was admitted to PGI on 10th
July 1972, Shipman being the admitting doctor. He made a diagnosis of myocardial infarction, and recorded a history of central chest pain radiating to the left arm. The findings on clinical examination were unremarkable, and routine investigations were arranged, including an ECG and blood tests. A lignocaine drip was started, suggesting that the patient was experiencing ectopic beats. He was making a reasonable recovery until a fever and tachycardia were noted on 20th
July. Later the same day he suffered rigors and Shipman prescribed medication, probably antibiotics (this is confirmed by the nursing notes). In the following week matters seemed to improve; some investigations of kidney function were performed without significant positive findings being brought to light.
July Mr Evans was seen by a doctor (not Shipman) because he had become unwell. The recorded features included sweating, pallor, some breathlessness and a rapid irregular pulse, but no pain. A cardiac monitor was set up to assist monitoring. Shipman was called at 8.48pm the same day because the patient was breathless. He recorded finding signs of heart failure (a gallop rhythm, crepitations at the lung bases, and a raised jugular venous pressure). The nursing notes indicate that the call for Shipman was at 8.40pm, using the term
‘crashed’
(i.e. a cardiac arrest). He started digoxin, Lasix and Slow-K.
At 6am the next day, the patient was recorded by another doctor as being very pale and sweaty; the blood pressure was not recordable, and there were crepitations in both bases. The lignocaine drip was stopped. Shipman saw him later in the day, and recorded his general condition (written in short hand as
‘G.C.’
) as poor; the blood pressure was still unrecordable. This clinical picture is confirmed by the nursing records. An entry in the nursing records states that at 6.45pm the patient had become breathless and Shipman had been informed. An injection of Pamergan P100 (i.e. pethidine) was given, and the patient was said to be in cardiogenic shock, and was
‘not to be crashed’
(i.e. would not be resuscitated if the heart stopped). The nursing record noted that Mr Evans was moved into a cubicle, probably indicating that his death was anticipated. At 11.15pm Shipman noted that the patient had collapsed with further chest pain, and had died. He states that the relatives were present (this is reiterated in the nursing records). There is no mention of attempted resuscitation, but he states
‘no need to report death’
. The cause of death was given as LVF due to a coronary thrombosis.
Observations
It is clear that, by the day of death, Mr Evans was expected to die. His relatives were in attendance and were present at his death. Although the records no longer contain an ECG, the diagnosis of a myocardial infarct appears appropriate, and is corroborated by the letter from the consultant to the GP after the patient’s death.

--- ADDENDUM TO PROFESSOR RICHARD BAKER'S REVIEW: DECEMBER 2004 ---
Distribution of Deaths by Time of Day
Chi Square

--- Distribution of Deaths by Time of Day ---
The following analyses have been undertaken to further explore the variation between doctors in the proportion of deaths reported as occurring at different times of the day. Table 1 compares all the cremation certificates completed by Shipman with all the remaining certificates combined. Shipman issued a total of 66 certificates (one of these related to a death that occurred outside PGI). The analysis points to a difference between Shipman’s certificates and the other certificates (the P values in this paper were all calculated using Chi square tests).
Table 1.All cremation certificates, Shipman and other doctors compared (P 0.009)
QUARTER
24-5.59
6-11.59
12-17.59
18-23.59
12.1%
27.3%
18.2%
42.4%
23.9%
25.0%
25.7%
25.4%
192
22.8%
25.2%
25.1%
26.9%
Table 1 does not deal with the issue of variability between doctors. There may be other doctors with patterns similar to that of Shipman but they would not have been highlighted by the analysis in Table 1. One hundred and ten doctors issued cremation certificates at the hospital during the years of interest. The details about the numbers certified by each doctor according to the time of day are not included here since the table would be very large. However, the findings can be summarised. The doctors issued between one and 66 certificates each, Shipman issuing the highest number. It is difficult to draw meaningful conclusions about the times of deaths reported by each doctor because many doctors reported only small numbers of deaths. A statistical test of these data indicated that the differences between the doctors in the times of deaths that they had reported were not statistically significant (P 0.179).
Table 2 presents information about the numbers of deaths in each quarter of the day reported by each doctor, but is restricted to those doctors who issued 20 or more certificates. The doctors are indicated by code numbers and not their names. The analysis indicates that the difference between these doctors is not statistically significant. It should be noted, however, that Shipman issued more certificates than any of the other doctors, and that the smaller the number of certificates, the greater the risk of variation.

The question is whether there was a large scatter in the distribution of timing between the comparison doctors. If there was - and if the distribution in the case of some was also unusual - then it may be that weight should not be attached to the finding that Shipman was unusual.
The analyses above are not very satisfactory since the comparison doctors did not issue as many certificates as Shipman. The original analysis compared Shipman to all the other doctors, and this demonstrated that the time distribution of deaths of Shipman’s patients was unusual. This finding stands. Shipman’s pattern was unusual. However, it is possible that the pattern of some other doctors was also unusual. The above tables indicate that no other doctor was as unusual as Shipman, although some doctors did report atypical patterns of times of deaths. Nevertheless, the small numbers of deaths certified by many of the doctors necessitates caution in drawing conclusions about each doctor.

--- Chi Square ---
Chi square is a particular statistical test. It is used in analyses of proportions or percentages, in particular to determine the probability that an observed difference between two samples in relation to a variable is due to chance. If two people toss a coin 100 times each, it would be expected that chance would ensure that each person ended up with around 50 heads and 50 tails. But if one person gets 30 heads and 70 tails, is this person doing something different or using a special coin?
The P value indicates the probability of the observed difference being significant, the point at which a proportion becomes significant usually being defined as the difference that would occur by chance in five or fewer occasions out of a hundred. Thus, a P value of 0.05 (i.e. 5%) is taken as significant (unlikely to be due to chance).
